[65] vision for Defence – 29 June 2016 Foreword by the Minister of Defence Dear Reader, My Government colleagues and I have favoured a positive vision of the future of Belgian Defence

[66] I firmly believe that accomplishing this strategic vision will ensure the security and freedom of all Belgians on a long-term basis and will largely contribute to our prosperity and well-being

[67] This vision of the future also strengthens the Euro-Atlantic anchoring of Belgian Defence

[68] Since the end of the Second World War this anchoring has guaranteed our security and that of all our European and trans-Atlantic fellow citizens

[69] In May 2015, I presented a strategic vision note to my Government colleagues on the future of Defence up to

[70] We immediately agreed on my analysis of the (future) Belgian security environment that you will find hereafter in the first chapter

[71] On this note, my collaborators, the experts of my fellow ministers, and the Defence Staff have worked hard on different possible scenarios for the future of Belgian Defence, inspired by ideas coming from the Parliament and the academic world

[72] On 22 December 2015, the Federal Government took the decisions of principle that were necessary to draw up this strategic vision and the Preamble by the Government accompanying this strategic vision

[73] I am proud of what we have achieved

[74] This vision honours the Government agreement which stated: “The Government will again provide the army with the means needed to perform its tasks properly.”

[75] A Defence of 25,000 full-time equivalents in 2030 has been chosen

[76] The defence personnel of the future will focus even more on the military specific tasks and as such the core business of Defence

[77] They will be increasingly supported by civilian service providers to do so

[78] combined with a strong rejuvenation, this evolution will boost the operational readiness of Defence

[79] The Government also opted for a budgetary path that must lead to a healthy balance between personnel costs, operating costs and investments in 2030 and that will release an investment budget of 9.4 billion euros for the Belgian military capabilities up to

[80] Our future capabilities will enable us to accomplish our multilateral solidarity and our will to contribute to a more European defence

[81] See Annex A. See Annex B. 2015 euros

[82] T h e S t r a t e

[83] i s i o n f o r D e f e n c e – 2 9 J u n e

[84] The decision path leading to this strategic vision for Defence proceeded step by step

[85] Taking into account the different political visions, we have succeeded in creating positive prospects for Defence and hence for its key stakeholder, i.e. the Belgian population, as well

[86] Investing in Defence is an intergenerational issue

[87] The decisions taken by this Government will also have an impact on the security of future generations

[88] This strategic vision offers the defence personnel the prospect of a modern and efficient organisation with opportunities for everyone

[89] It offers the prospect of a Defence able to maintain a broadly deployable toolbox for the purpose of the defence policy of current and future Governments

[90] It offers the prospect of a Defence based on multilateral solidarity towards NATO, the EU and the UN

[91] It offers the prospect of a Defence committed to supporting the building of a more European defence

[92] And last but not least, the strategic vision offers the prospect of a Defence which plays an important role in defending the security, the freedom, the prosperity and the well-being of all Belgian citizens

[93] Once again I would like to express my sincere thanks to everybody who helped in bringing about this historical turning point for Defence: my fellow politicians, members of Parliament, my Defence Staff, academics, the members of my private office

[94] During this term of office we will start the practical realisation of this vision which will also remain a guide for future Governments

[95] Finally, I wish you a pleasant reading of the strategic vision for Belgian Defence towards

[97] Introduction This strategic vision is based on a general framework, namely a Defence of 25,000 Full Time Equivalents (FTE) and a budget for investments in major equipment of 9.4 billion euros4 (2015 euros) until

[98] Furthermore, this vision is built around 9 principles described in Annex A. Besides the analysis of our security environment (approved in May 2015) which clearly points out the security challenges for our country, these principles form the guiding thread of this strategic vision

[99] The first chapter of this vision thus includes the analysis of the security environment with updated figures

[100] This document determines the capability portfolio for our Defence towards 2030, within the investment budget and the personnel envelope

[101] Three intermediary chapters, each of them structured around a number of specific principles, are the steppingstones from the security environment to the description of the future capabilities

[102] Chapter two highlights principles no. 1 and 3a

[103] Principle no. 1 clearly determines the core tasks of Defence, namely collective defence, collective security and the protection of Belgian citizens all over the world

[104] Principle no. 3a underlines that the capabilities of Belgian Defence should allow it to perform the core tasks in an appropriate way

[105] Chapter 2 thus sketches the outlines for the capabilities starting from the different core tasks our Defence has to carry out in the security environment

[106] In order to carry out the supporting role of Defence within the framework of internal security, it is important that Defence strives to intensify cooperation with the national security actors

[107] This will strengthen interdepartmental efficiency on the national territory (principle no

[108] This chapter is dedicated to the optimisation of this support

[109] Chapter three addresses the political will to maintain a pioneering role for a more European defence

[110] This willingness is to be found in principle no. 3c which states that our country should 4 All figures referred to in this document are in constant 2015 euros

[111] According to NATO, a military capability includes the following Lines of Development: doctrine (how to employ military means, e.g. the combined air-ground engagement during a military campaign), organisation (organisation in the event of engagement, e.g. a combined arms Battlegroup), training (how the operational preparation is done for these means), materiel (all material aspects needed by the military forces in order to operate effectively, e.g. weapons and spare parts), leadership and education (how superiors are trained for operations at different levels within the armed forces and the necessary training in order to engage personnel and deploy materiel), personnel (availability of qualified personnel for engagement and support), infrastructure (immovable property like military quarters and training facilities for supporting the armed forces) and interoperability (the possibility to engage our own armed forces with those of other NATO member states

[112] This strategic vision is based on 25,000 Full Time Equivalents (FTE) and a budget for investments in major equipment of 9.4 billion euros (2015 euros) until

[113] T h e S t r a t e

[114] i s i o n f o r D e f e n c e – 2 9 J u n e 2 0 1 6 participate in filling European capability gaps

[115] Principle no. 6 follows suit, as it proposes elaborate forms of international cooperation in support of a more European defence policy

[116] The security environment clearly shows the need for a greater European military autonomy, both in matters of policy and in the field of capabilities

[117] This ambition has immediately visible consequences for the future capability portfolio of Defence

[118] Chapter four describes the importance of a recapitalization of Defence that is based on multilateral solidarity, and the way it will improve the internal budgetary balance of the defence effort by

[119] It is essential that Defence should be given sufficient investment budgets if it wants to further take part in generating multilateral security in the future

[120] In addition to this, the necessary operational resources and personnel budgets should be released in order to sufficiently contribute to the multilateral Euro-Atlantic burden-sharing within the framework of collective defence and collective security

[121] Principles no. 2, 3b and 3d hint at this need when underlining the necessity for a defence budget based on multilateral solidarity for a capability output in line with multilateral solidarity

[122] They also refer to a balanced internal costs structure for Defence

[123] Chapter five details the capability portfolio by

[124] A balance between the military capabilities, based on the three core tasks, is defined in principle no. 3a

[125] b then provides for a balance among the four capability dimensions (intelligence-cyber-influence, land, air, maritime) with at least one credible and sustainably deployable combat capability per dimension

[126] c introduces additional equilibriums by also laying stress on non-kinetic and civil- military capabilities

[127] The emphasis put on the strategic support capabilities of this capability portfolio – which, most of the time, correspond to European capability gaps (principle no. 3c) – has already been mentioned above

[128] With this capability portfolio, Defence has a clear heading for the long term, towards

[129] The following chapters will give the necessary guidance for the implementation of this strategic vision, based on the other principles

[130] Chapter six sets out the guidelines for a continuous pursuit of an efficient Defence (principle no

[131] The functioning of Defence will be rationalised as much as possible by building structures that follow the processes, and not the other way around

[132] For Defence, the key process is: generating military capabilities for the execution of operations within the framework of the three core tasks

[133] Defence should thus focus on military specific tasks and as much as possible make use of market opportunities for supporting tasks and services within the framework of a sourcing policy

[134] Capability gaps are the shortages at the European level for certain defence capabilities that complicate autonomous European military engagement

[135] An action is efficient if the efforts and expenses concerned make an effective contribution to the achievement of the objective (= effective) and if, at the same time, the costs are proportionate to the profits

[136] This document determines the capability portfolio for our Defence towards 2030, within the investment budget and the personnel envelope

[137] T h e S t r a t e

[138] i s i o n f o r D e f e n c e – 2 9 J u n e 2 0 1 6 This chapter also pays attention to a continued rationalisation of the competence development and the military medical support

[139] As stated in principle no. 4, this strategic vision also enables a further rationalisation of the infrastructure

[140] Another possibility of improving our national security policy is to take advantage of the existing and developing links between Defence, the national research institutions and the industry for the increased support of our security interests

[141] Defence can thus strengthen the efficiency of the security policy

[142] As mentioned in principle no. 8, this objective can enable the transfer of know-how, technology and employment to our society

[143] Chapter seven brings forward the ambition with regard to personnel, i.e. to rejuvenate Defence by developing a more flexible personnel policy

[144] This chapter is completely built around principle no. 9 which asks to assess the education/training and the personnel regulations of servicemen, and to consider which adjustments bring about a more dynamic personnel policy

[145] The aim is to abandon the principle of Lifetime Employment as much as possible in order to make Defence a younger organisation and therefore better adapted to its core business

[146] The creation of a modern Reserve is an integral part of the efforts provided to strive for a more flexible personnel policy

[147] Obviously, this ambition regarding personnel should be further developed

[148] The social partners should be involved as much as possible, not only as representatives of the Defence personnel members, but also as experts on these questions

[149] Finally, chapter eight provides the steps for the implementation of this strategic vision

[150] This will be achieved through an implementation plan for the geographical distribution of the quarters

[151] This plan will be valid for the duration of the strategic vision

[152] A proposition for the military programme Act which ensures the legal anchoring of the investment programmes for major equipment for the duration of this strategic vision is also planned in the four months following the strategic vision (see principle no

[153] Furthermore, this strategic vision forms the foundation on which a series of individual projects will be elaborated and coordinated at the political level with the aim to build together and step by step the future of Defence

[155] The Belgian Security Environment up to

[156] Continuously Uncertain and More Complex The future always remains uncertain

[157] The rapidly increasing complexity of the world is, however, remarkable

[158] This is due to the exponentially rising pace of sociological, economic, political, environmental and technological change

[159] This far more complex world is an important factor in defining the place Belgium wants to occupy in the global security environment

[160] Through its composition, Belgian Defence needs to be able to adapt quickly and flexibly to this environment

[161] Today’s multipolar world will persist in the years to come, as new poles of power will equally take their place on the world stage

[162] At the moment, this transition period is already causing an increasing amount of friction

[163] New poles of power, but also a growing number of non-state actors try to (re)define their role in the current system of global governance

[164] The community of states is thus going through a period of change which can generate uncertainties and even instability

[165] In a more fragmented world, regional security dynamics will become more important again

[166] The Europe of NATO and EU countries, already an important economic pole today, needs to extend its policy instruments in this multipolar world, as to be not merely a pawn but a player in this new global constellation

[167] The regional European framework is already the most important dimension of the Belgian security policy, primarily through the multilateral framework of NATO and the EU, but additionally also that of the OSCE (Organisation for Security and Cooperation in Europe

[168] The United States are Europe’s most important global security partner

[169] They ask the European countries for a more important contribution to the stabilisation of the European periphery

[170] Europe will increasingly have to take up the role of a security provider instead of being a security consumer

[171] The United States are indeed progressively shifting their focus to the security situation in Asia, as it can have a more profound effect on the status of the US as a global power than the friction in the European periphery

[172] This observation only reinforces the need for the European countries to become a more autonomous military security player in and around the European continent

[173] The Europe of NATO and EU countries needs to extend its policy instruments in this multipolar world, as to be not merely a pawn but a player in this new global constellation

[174] The European countries need to become a more autonomous military security player in and around the European continent

[175] T h e S t r a t e

[176] Now more than ever, security in both Belgium and Europe is connected with peace and security in the world

[177] Non-military means play a central role in the promotion of a tolerant, safe and prosperous world

[178] However, Defence remains a necessary tool in the states’ toolbox

[179] All security experts are in agreement on this

[180] The military instrument is necessary to protect not just the Belgian society and interests, but also the universal values anchored in the European DNA

[181] Because of these values and Europe’s economic prosperity, the European societal model remains attractive to other countries and their population

[182] In contrast however, extremists can influence the Belgian and European population directly and attack the values on which this open and tolerant Belgian and European society is built

[183] Extremists are by definition prepared to use extreme means and in this context, the threat of military retaliation remains a very useful power tool

[184] Europe: an Island of Peace Surrounded by a Periphery in Chaos Especially its economic but also its social and political integration make Europe – and particularly the EU – the most important reference for the Belgian international security policy

[185] Reality today is that this Europe in peace is surrounded by a periphery in chaos

[186] I. The European Southern Periphery

[187] The terrorist organisation IS manages to execute large-scale and cross-border military operations in Syria and Iraq which attract Western radicalised youths

[188] These young people are a threat to our internal security

[189] T h e S t r a t e

[190] The Arab Spring has led to a clear deterioration of the security environment south of Europe and in the Middle East

[191] What started as a wave of democratisation based on universal values did not result in a lasting emancipation of the population

[192] For a long time to come, these regions will probably be plagued by cross-border extremist groups, domestic and regional conflicts, a social-economic situation with no prospect of improvement and weak and/or authoritarian regimes with limited internal and external legitimacy

[193] According to the Fragile States Index this southern European periphery, consisting of North Africa, the Sahel, the Horn of Africa and the Middle East, includes the most fragile countries in the world

[194] The EU and the European countries need to continue to play a role in the stabilisation of these countries, if necessary by using military means

[195] Moreover, the societal instability in these countries also has a direct negative impact on the security, well-being and prosperity of European citizens

[196] This is the spillover of international terrorism, piracy, international crime (trafficking in drugs, arms and human beings), illegal immigration and refugee flows

[197] However, instability is not inevitable

[198] In Chad for instance, the European Union has shown that the combination of military intervention and the development of administrative capacities can provide a permanent solution to long-lasting conflicts

[199] In Libya by contrast, a successful military action of the European countries in 2011 was not followed by an equally bold civil-military stabilisation phase

[200] This failure led to the destabilisation of not only Libya, but also large parts of the region (including Mali

[201] The conflict in Syria provides the clearest illustration of the link between external and internal security, namely the participation of radicalised foreign fighters in this conflict and their subsequent return to Europe

[202] This conflict also shows that non-intervention carries a cost at the national and European level

[203] Furthermore, the stability of the southern European periphery will be subjected to more pressure in the years to come because of the impact of demographic and climatic evolutions

[204] The entire region will experience a strong population growth over the next few years

[205] This will be especially the case in West Africa

[206] The region’s fragile ecosystems will have even more difficulties in dealing with this demographic growth, especially given the additional climatic impact on this region

[207] Local authorities do not have sufficient means nor resilience to carry out the necessary adjustments

[208] Without sufficient support from Europe to allow them to adapt to changing climatic circumstances local tensions will rise because of the additional pressure on food security, water security and energy security

[209] This reality increases the likelihood of military stabilisation missions in this region in the future

[210] The Fragile States Index 2015 (http://fsi.fundforpeace.org/rankings

[211] The stability of the southern European periphery will be subjected to more pressure in the years to come

[212] T h e S t r a t e

[213] The Strategic Vision for Defence – 29 June 2016 Within the framework of the Common Foreign and Security Policy, the EU pays particular attention to reinforcing security and establishing stable governance in the southern periphery

[214] NATO is also clear about the importance of this region for the Euro-Atlantic security, which is why it has invested in regional partnerships over the past decades

[215] Conventional deterrence supports European diplomacy and other preventive efforts here as well

[216] Although Central Africa borders on the southern European periphery, the direct impact of this region on European security is rather limited today

[217] Nevertheless, a direct link between this region and our country remains because of the presence of a significant diaspora from this region in our country

[218] The Great Lakes region is the centre of political and military friction in Central Africa, and the Democratic Republic of the Congo remains one of the most fragile states of this region

[219] The Belgian government wants to continue to contribute to the stabilisation of Central Africa and of the Great Lakes region in particular, through a national comprehensive approach, in which Defence also plays a structural role

[220] The Belgian expertise in this region allows our diplomacy as well as our intelligence services to build up credit on a global level

[221] This credit can then be used for other issues that have an impact on the Belgian and European security interests

[222] The situation in the eastern periphery of Europe is also back on the security agenda, in a way that we have not seen since the end of the Cold War

[223] The interference of Russia in the east of the Ukraine, the annexation of Crimea, and the earlier disinformation and military actions in Transnistria and Georgia confirm the fact that Russia does not resign itself to the expanding NATO/EU sphere of influence even though this expansion is merely reflecting the sovereign wish of the former Warsaw Pact countries and former Soviet Union republics to join NATO and the EU

[224] Russia’s attitude in the eastern European periphery will determine Europe’s security and military position towards Russia for a long time

[225] NATO has made a clear response by refocusing on its collective defence task

[226] After the Cold War this task had lost its 9 As indicated in the declaration of the NATO summit in Wales (NATO

[251] The East European countries expect the other NATO member states – including Belgium – to increase their military presence in the East and to maintain a sufficiently high contribution to the burden and risk sharing of military deterrence

[252] In recent years, Belgian Defence has actively contributed to this, notably by participating in the air defence in Eastern Europe, but also by carrying out mine-clearing activities in the Baltic Sea and participating in NATO exercises in this region with our land forces

[253] NATO is developing a renewed policy in Eastern Europe, specifically for the land forces, aimed at provisioning and prepositioning heavier means, as was the case for a long time in Central Europe during the Cold War

[254] Hybrid warfare combines military and non-military means and methods to destabilise countries

[255] Russia, for instance, uses hybrid warfare in the Ukraine and Georgia by supporting opponents of the local government with civilian and military means and by using military and civilian elements of power, such as the media and the economy, to the detriment of another country

[256] Therefore, hybrid warfare is also regarded as a negative form of the well known comprehensive approach of the EU, which uses an appropriate combination of civilian and military means to stabilise areas within the framework of crisis management

[257] The roles of NATO and the EU are thus very much complementary in the reinforcement of the integrity of their East European member states against Russian interference

[260] The European Northern Periphery Due to global warming, the strategic relevance of the Arctic region could increase significantly by 2030, as shipping routes through the Arctic Ocean will become easier to navigate and the reserves of energy and other raw materials present in this region will become more accessible

[261] In time, this would mean for Belgian ports that the shipping routes to East Asia will become shorter: by 40% to Japan, 30% to Korea and 20 to 25% to the important Chinese ports, compared to the current route through the Suez Canal

[262] Climatologists estimate that by 2030 the entire Arctic Ocean will be ice-free by the end of summer, which would shorten the distances even further

[263] Russia has already started to reinforce its governance in this region, also by reopening military bases

[264] Several other Arctic countries are also developing their capability to reinforce their governance

[265] Still, most specialists assume that for the time being the northern European periphery will remain quiet as far as security is concerned

[266] NATO and EU: Key Pillars of the Belgian Security and Defence Policy After the failing of the Belgian neutrality policy during both World Wars of the twentieth century, Belgium became one of the founders of the post-war security architecture, based on multilateralism, in which – besides the UN – NATO and the EU take up a prominent place

[267] Belgium occupies a central position within NATO and the EU as host country of the main institutions of both organisations, such as the NATO headquarters and SHAPE (Supreme Headquarters Allied Powers Europe), NATO’s most important military command

[268] Brussels is also de facto the capital of the European Union because of the presence of the main EU institutions

[269] The important link between Belgium and both NATO and the EU testifies to the desire of the Belgian authorities to safeguard the security of Belgium through multilateral anchoring with allies that are closely related ideologically and geographically

[270] In an uncertain world, with a growing number of security threats at the borders of Europe, both organisations will undoubtedly continue to play a main role in the safeguarding of European security for the next fifteen years

[271] The Belgian authorities consider NATO and the EU to be complementary organisations

[272] They underline the necessity for a greater integration of the European defence effort to support a credible and balanced NATO as well as the possibility for the EU to carry out The Belgian statesman Paul-Henri Spaak became Secretary General of NATO in

[284] Flow security is aimed at the protection of “flows” (of goods, raw materials, people, capital, services, and information) that are not constrained by territoriality anymore, but do have a link with the interests of a country or group of countries and their economy and society

[285] A global flow – such as international maritime transport – is not a new phenomenon, but it becomes all the more important in a globalised economy

[286] Consequently, the protection of maritime supply lines is directly connected with the performance of the Belgian and European economies

[287] In 2015 Belgium was ranked 19th in the world by tonnage of the commercial fleet controlled by Belgian companies and persons

[288] Belgium was thus ranked higher than Russia, the Netherlands, and France

[289] Given its high economic importance, Belgium has to contribute proportionally to the global protection and safeguarding of the maritime flow

[290] In our multipolar world no country benefits from disrupting these global flows

[291] However, military conflicts arising from the fragmentation of the political global governance can have a negative influence on these global flows in the decades to come

[292] For example, several countries in East and Southeast Asia, particularly China and its neighbours, are investing in submarines on a massive scale, in order to be able to claim access to maritime areas and deny it to adversaries should a conflict erupt

[293] Flow security also consists of relatively new areas, such as the protection of the cyber environment

[294] This environment has not only become a part of everyday life, closely linked to the physical and social well-being of the Belgian and European population, it has also become the backbone of the Belgian and European economy

[295] As an essential part of the worldwide economic and social tissue, the cyber environment has more and more security consequences

[296] The use of this environment by security services and as an additional dimension for (the support of) warfare is the most direct link between security and the cyber environment, and it should also be taken into account

[309] The United Nations under Pressure After the Second World War, the United Nations were mandated with a central role in maintaining global security and regulating the use of the military power instrument

[310] The Government agreement states that the Belgian government wishes to contribute in a credible and lasting way to this role of the United Nations, given its direct impact on Belgian security and interests

[311] From a military point of view this implies a continued significant deployment of our Defence in UN mandated military operations, including UN-mandated NATO and EU operations

[312] However, it is troubling that the position of the UN as central organisation for peace and security in the world has come under pressure

[313] On the one hand, this is due to the fact that the new emerging powers are focusing more on their own regional spheres of influence, which reinforces the fragmentation of the world into regional poles

[314] On the other hand, there is an imbalance between the new geopolitical realities and the current representativeness in the United Nations Security Council

[315] The development of a multipolar world could lead to friction and armed conflicts in overlapping areas of influence, but also an open conflict between the new poles cannot be excluded

[316] It is necessary to pay due attention to the fact that the emerging powers are investing heavily in their military power

[317] Whereas the European defence effort has decreased substantially in recent years, that of the BRIC countries (Brazil, Russia, India and China) increased by 38 to 132% between 2006 and

[318] Three of these countries have nuclear weapons

[327] The Strategic Vision for Defence – c. Defence as the Last Bulwark of Belgian Internal Security The growing influence of international security issues on our internal security is first of all dealt with by reinforcing international security

[328] However, ensuring security on the national territory should not be overlooked

[329] Thus, Defence is preferably deployed within the framework of common international operations in order to prevent an actual threat on the national territory

[330] However, Defence also is and remains a security actor on the national territory, inter alia for specialised tasks such as air defence, maritime patrols, or the clearance of explosives from both world wars and of parcel bombs, in particular within the framework of counter-terrorism

[352] This safeguards our national security in a multipolar world in a relevant way

[355] A Balanced Positioning of Belgian Defence in the Security Environment Defence is an important actor that meets the need for security identified by the analysis of the security environment

[356] Moreover, Defence is an essential instrument for a credible foreign and security policy

[357] “A modern Defence remains one of the crucial components of our security and an absolute necessity to conduct a credible foreign and security policy.” (Government agreement of 9 October

[358] The essence of our Defence is to contribute to the security of our citizens and the protection of our values and worldwide interests

[359] These form the basis for our freedom, well-being and prosperity

[360] Because of the strong European anchoring of our interests and values, the national and European security policies are inextricably linked

[361] Defence contributes to international security in order to prevent existing threats from having a negative effect on our society and new threats against our territory from originating

[362] Defence also contributes to the protection of global flows, which are of vital importance to our open economy and thus to the prosperity and well-being of the population

[363] Starting from the analysis of the security environment and the core tasks of Defence, this chapter describes which position our Defence needs to occupy by 2030 in order to ensure our security, prosperity and well-being

[364] The analysis of the security environment and the input of Belgian Defence experts have shown several possible core tasks that Defence should carry out in the future

[365] Three core tasks have been selected, i.e.: * contribute to the collective defence through NATO to defend the Alliance’s territorial integrity; Collective defence against an armed attack violating the territorial integrity of the NATO member states in Europe and North America is at the very heart of the 1949 NATO treaty and is enshrined in Article

[366] According to this article an attack against one of the NATO member states shall be considered an attack on all member states, which can, if they deem it necessary, use armed force to restore the territorial integrity

[367] By ensuring interoperability between NATO forces, by striving for sufficient and adequate military capabilities in the member states, by joint training and by joint planning, NATO provides an effective collective defence with military means

[368] contribute to the collective security18 through crisis management operations19 in a multilateral or international framework, preferably mandated or organised by international security organisations, to ensure peace and security in the world; * protect Belgian citizens all over the world

[369] Collective security consists in ensuring peace and (physical) security within the framework of (potentially) armed conflicts

[370] This takes place outside the Belgian borders and usually outside NATO and EU borders

[371] Collective security is directed at security crises that do not directly threaten our territorial integrity or that of our allies, contrary to collective defence, but that (can) endanger our security and our interests (and those of our allies

[372] These interests also include the pursuit and maintenance of an international order based on rules that protect the safety of a population

[373] Different military and civilian means and ways exist to contribute to collective security, e.g. through civil-military crisis management operations, diplomacy (including defence diplomacy), development cooperation and disarmament

[374] The core task of Belgian Defence specifically consists of a contribution to the collective security through crisis management operations

[375] Crisis management operations are operations with civilian and/or military means to prevent a security crisis from escalating into a conflict, and to end or (help) to settle an ongoing conflict that can have an impact on the security and interests of our country and our allies

[376] Crisis management operations can also take place within the context of the Responsibility to Protect a population that is being exposed to (an important threat of) physical violence, which goes against maintaining a rules-based international order

[377] Crisis management operations can also contribute to the consolidation of stability in post-conflict situations

[378] The more restrictive definition used in this document does not consider deployments within the framework of collective defence or international humanitarian operations through military means to be crisis management operations

[379] Military deployment within the framework of crisis management operations can involve conflict prevention, preventive military deployments, peacekeeping, peacemaking, peacebuilding and peace enforcement

[380] On 25 February 2015 the Minister of Defence organised a seminar in support of the development of the strategic vision

[381] The Minister asked a group of experts from the academic, diplomatic, financial and economic world as well as from the media and the military to give their vision on the tasks and capabilities of Defence for the period until

[383] Defence can also always be deployed by the government within the framework of national and international missions according to the capabilities available.”

[405] The Appropriate Balance Between the Core Tasks ‘Collective Defence’ and ‘Collective Security’ for the Contribution of Belgian Defence to Security in the European Periphery and the World The term ‘collective’ in ‘collective defence’ and ‘collective security’ refers to the fact that both core tasks are essentially a European and often a Euro-Atlantic issue

[406] If our Defence wants to make a relevant contribution to European security – which is the broader frame of our national security – all Belgian defence capabilities have to contribute to these core tasks in an appropriate way

[407] ‘Collective security’ concerns a contribution to stability outside the national borders (and those of our allies), in places that matter to our own security

[408] By contrast, the focus of ‘collective defence’ is on defending the national and allied territory and on preventing a violation of the territorial integrity through military deterrence

[409] The analysis of our security environment shows that Europe can expect even more security threats, especially in its direct eastern and southern periphery

[410] In the future these two areas will be the primary focus of European, including the Belgian, armed forces

[411] The security environment analysis has shown two significantly different theatres, in which the threat requires a different approach

[412] However, in both theatres, only a collective European and transatlantic approach can create the desired security

[413] In the north collective peace will probably prevail until 2030, but after that it is possible that collective vigilance will be required for this European peripheral region as well

[414] The purpose of defence diplomacy is to forge a bond of trust between different countries through several military trust-building actions, such as exchanges of military personnel, visits of ships and aircraft, contacts between defence staffs, joint training and exercises

[415] This additional task will be performed by the available capabilities of Defence, and can for instance take place during the operational preparation phase

[434] The security dynamic in the southern European periphery is experiencing a negative development

[435] Up until today, the spillover of insecurity from this region has not posed a direct threat to the European territorial integrity, but it does have an impact on internal security

[436] The EU (through the Common Security and Defence Policy), the UN and separate European countries – either within the framework of a coalition of the willing or not – are the most active actors in this southern region, alongside regional security organisations and local national actors

[437] They contribute to the reinforcement of international security by means of civilian and military crisis management

[438] Recently NATO has also again underlined its growing concern about the southern flank

[439] Within the framework of collective security, Defence is an active policy instrument that is preferably deployed to intervene before an armed conflict actually breaks out or before failed states emerge and with them armed groups who can threaten our security or interests

[440] In the last few years, these types of deployment of our Defence to prevent (or stop the development of) security crises have occurred in the Central African Republic, Niger, the Palestinian territories, the Democratic Republic of the Congo,…

[441] These engagements notably included assistance in building a secure society and the development of national security actors who are accountable for their actions

[442] When in spite of all this armed conflicts still break out, interventions can be carried out within the framework of collective security to stop them and subsequently contribute to the building of a stable and secure society, in coordination with other international and interdepartmental actors

[443] The operations in Chad, Mali, Somalia, the Gulf of Aden, Syria, and Iraq are just a few examples of these types of deployment

[444] Interventions can be carried out within the framework of collective security to stop an armed conflict and subsequently contribute to the building of a stable and secure society, in coordination with other international and interdepartmental actors

[445] The Strategic Vision for Defence – 29 June 2016 The analysis of the security environment indicates that this southern region has a continuing and even growing need for European crisis management, with or without military support

[446] In collective security Defence is but one of the actors in a joint approach with civilian actors (comprehensive approach

[447] Defence only takes the lead in an intervention to effectively stop an armed conflict

[448] Stabilisation can take place by providing assistance in ending a conflict, by providing security, and by contributing to the start-up of the reconstruction after the military part of a conflict has ended (follow-up phase, reconstruction

[449] At that moment, the security situation is often still too precarious for civilian relief organisations

[450] Intelligence services will then play an important role in determining the necessity to intervene and in understanding the best way in which to proceed

[452] The effectiveness of the military deployment depends on the extent to which Defence can cooperate with other actors

[453] This comprehensive approach does not only have an international dimension (primarily through the EU and the UN), but also a national interdepartmental dimension

[454] According to the current security analysis and its expected future evolution it does not seem appropriate for Belgian Defence to focus exclusively on collective defence – as it did during the Cold War

[460] The analysis of our security environment describes how a change in the global positioning of the United States forces the European countries to take more autonomous military action in their own periphery

[461] In the future, the European countries will be less able to call on the strategic support capabilities of the United States such as tanker aircraft, large transport aircraft, reconnaissance drones, long-range precision missiles, Special Operations Forces, offensive cyber capabilities and intelligence gathering

[462] However, those capabilities are essential for, among other things, crisis management operations at the borders of Europe

[463] This means that the European countries will have to invest more in these capability gaps in order to obtain the necessary autonomy

[473] The Air Combat Capability: a Spearhead for Collective Defence At the moment, the spearhead of our contribution to NATO’s collective defence is the air combat capability, assured with the F-16-multirole combat aircraft

[474] This capability is particularly appropriate for collective defence, since these aircraft can be deployed quickly and over large distances

[475] This capability contributes to the territorial dimension of collective defence on the Belgian (and soon the Benelux) territory by means of aircraft on Quick Reaction Alert as well as to NATO’s broader deterrence through, among other things, air defence of the Alliance borders

[476] But for our air combat capability to be able to continue to contribute to NATO’s deterrence in an effective and credible way, it is essential that this is done with high-tech air assets that offer a lasting advantage over the combat aircraft of possible adversaries

[477] Therefore, we comply with the request of NATO to acquire modern combat aircraft, which are technologically more advanced and designed to integrate developing technologies over the next decades

[478] These aircraft need to be available to be deployed for NATO’s collective defence for the coming decades

[479] Multirole: the possibility to use a platform to execute both air defence and air attack missions, as well as ground attack missions, reconnaissance and direct support of ground troops

[480] Belgian F-16 over Lithuania

[481] For several years our combat aircraft have been ensuring the air defence of the Baltic States within the framework of NATO

[482] For our air combat capability to be able to continue to contribute to NATO’s deterrence in an effective and credible way, it is essential that this is done with high-tech combat aircraft

[483] The 34 new modern combat aircraft also need to be multirole so that they can be deployed in the best possible way to ensure security at the European borders within the framework of collective security

[484] The efficient deployment of an air combat capability strongly depends on tanker aircraft, both for collective defence and for collective security

[485] In the past, European combat aircraft could always count on the United States’ air refuelling capability

[486] As a result, Europe has developed only a limited capability itself

[487] At the moment, this essential operational logistic capability of the combined European defence forces is too limited to allow an autonomous European deployment with combat aircraft at the borders of Europe

[488] Several European countries have taken action, for instance through the European Defence Agency (EDA), to fill this capability gap

[489] Therefore, it has been decided that our country will reinforce the European air-to-air refuelling capability through a contribution to a European pool of tanker aircraft corresponding to the capability of one tanker aircraft and through the acquisition of refuelling kits for the future A400M transport aircraft

[490] This will be done within the framework of EDA programmes for the reinforcement of this capability

[491] At the Transatlantic level Defence will also continue to contribute to NATO’s AWACS command and control aircraft, which form a strategic support capability for the deployment of our combat aircraft, both within the framework of collective defence and collective security

[492] Furthermore, AWACS aircraft are also essential for intelligence gathering and for airspace surveillance

[493] The Navy: a Second Spearhead for Collective Defence The Belgian ports are European external borders within the framework of European security

[494] They are essential to an effective organisation of collective defence at NATO level

[495] Therefore, our ports and the connected European sea lines of communication have to be kept free of mines at all times

[496] To this end, the current mine hunters will be replaced by six new mine countermeasures vessels equipped with an adequate mine countermeasures toolbox, which will allow a future-oriented concept of use and will also easily respond to the new technological evolutions of the coming decades

[497] The new ships will offer not only the accomplishment of the national tasks within the framework of collective defence but also a contribution to the expeditionary deployment within an international framework in order to keep the European maritime supply routes open

[498] Belgian Defence thus maintains a capability for which it has an internationally recognised expertise and that will remain relevant in the future

[499] The Strategic Vision for Defence – 29 June 2016 The Surface Combatant capability (based on of multi-purpose frigates) is important for the protection of maritime supply routes that are indispensable to the Belgian and European economy

[500] However, today our current frigates are insufficiently equipped

[501] The submarine threat is growing in the European periphery as well as all over the world, and NATO is requesting that additional efforts be made by the European member states

[502] The purpose is to better equip our current frigates for anti-submarine warfare and for the monitoring of European maritime supply routes that also have a global security dimension

[503] That is why their capability will be reinforced in the short term by the integration of new, already acquired NH90 frigate helicopters as a combat capability aboard these ships

[504] To this end, these helicopters will be equipped with modules adapted to the engagement against submarines and surface ships

[505] It has also been decided to replace the two current frigates with new ships of the same type that will be the spearhead of our contribution to NATO’s collective defence in the future

[506] In 2015, the Belgian frigate ‘Leopold I’ participated in the protection of the French aircraft carrier ‘Charles de Gaulle’ from which combat aircraft performed missions over Iraq and Syria

[507] The Surface Combatant capability (based on multi-purpose frigates) is important for the protection of maritime supply routes that are indispensable to the Belgian and European economy

[508] Just as the new combat aircraft, these new multirole frigates will also be deployed in missions within the framework of collective security, such as the expeditionary protection of supply routes or counter-piracy missions

[509] These tasks aim to protect the maritime interests of our country and play a part in the protection of Belgian citizens on board our merchant fleet, which is an important fleet at a global level as shown in the analysis of the strategic environment

[510] It will be examined whether our Navy can participate in the functioning of the Dutch Joint Logistic Support Ship (JSS) in order to be able to offer an additional contribution to the operational logistic support of the Admiral Benelux fleet (ABNL) and to the autonomous deployment of the Belgian and European maritime capabilities

[511] This operational logistic support is essential to the reinforcement of the effectiveness of the combat capability, i.e. the frigates

[512] In consultation with the Netherlands, this platform also offers the possibility to have a medical Role 2 on board and to support land operations from the sea, such as evacuation operations

[513] Civilian and military ships are most vulnerable when they are moored

[514] In port they are an easy target for actions that can be very effective with limited means

[515] NATO has stressed the importance of developing specific capabilities against this threat

[516] Based on the existing knowledge within NATO, Belgian Defence will develop a harbour protection capability, which can make a significant contribution to the maritime deployment, within the framework of both collective defence and collective security, in an expeditionary way as well as for our own ports

[517] The Optimisation of Land Forces for Motorised Deployment and Special Operations Given the renewed importance of collective defence, NATO is bringing the focus back to mechanised land forces with tracked vehicles and high firepower

[518] However, taking into account the budgetary context, the reality regarding personnel and the large number of necessary investments in all capability dimensions, it is not realistic for Belgium to redevelop a heavy land capability by 2030, while keeping the current motorised land forces

[519] The government has explicitly opted to maintain and modernise the existing motorised land forces so that they can remain the spearhead of the Belgian deployment within the context of collective security

[520] See paragraph ‘medical support’ in chapter

[521] Mechanised land forces with tracked vehicles have the best protection, the highest firepower and a very good tactical mobility (in combat

[522] Disadvantages are their reduced operational mobility and the fact that many of these systems are difficult to transport by air

[523] See chapter 5, section paragraph i. on the combined arms motorised capability in which the specificities of this type of land forces are explained

[524] The motorised land forces as the spearhead of the Belgian deployment within the context of collective security

[525] This strategic vision will allow a complete re-equipment of the Belgian motorised land forces, for manoeuvre, combat support as well as combat service support elements

[526] This equipment is necessary for their operational preparation and deployment, and to meet the desired level of ambition

[527] This equipment is just as necessary to ensure the individual safety of the servicemen and to take a step toward a more networked deployment

[528] Because of the optimisation of its vehicles in terms of protection and deployability, our motorised capability based on wheeled vehicles is specifically suited for operations within the framework of collective security

[529] Motorised land forces fully meet the deployment conditions for crisis management operations in the southern European periphery (North Africa, Sahel, Middle East

[530] Thus, for the duration of this strategic vision the focus will primarily be on flexibly deployable land forces to ensure stability and security in this southern European periphery, and secondarily, on interventions worldwide within the same context of crisis management

[531] Additionally, these motorised land forces can also be deployed within the framework of rapid interventions against hybrid threats, supporting collective defence within the framework of NATO

[532] It will thus remain possible for the existing and future Belgian land forces to make a meaningful contribution to the NATO stand-by forces within the framework of collective defence (NATO Response Force (NRF) and Very High Readiness Joint Task Force (VJTF

[533] Furthermore, while staying within the current budgetary path and the planned means for investing in major equipment, it is also possible to widen the deployment possibilities of our para-commando troops within the framework of a general reinforcement of our Special Operations Forces capability, a capability which is more and more demanded by NATO, especially when dealing with hybrid threats

[534] The two current para-commando battalions will be transformed into two Ranger battalions which will primarily focus on the support of the Special Forces, as Special Forces Support Group

[535] Today the para-commando troops are primarily airborne units

[536] Belgian motorised land forces ensure the safe conduct of the European Union Training Mission in Mali with their Dingo vehicles

[537] This strategic vision will allow a complete re-equipment of the Belgian motorised land forces

[538] The two current para-commando battalions will be transformed into two Ranger battalions which will primarily focus on the support of the Special Forces, as Special Forces Support Group

[539] The Strategic Vision for Defence – 29 June

[540] The transformation of the para-commandos into Rangers in support of the Special Forces widens their deployment possibilities, ensures the lasting relevance of these units in the future, and considerably reinforces the Belgian deployable capabilities in a NATO and EU context

[541] Besides the reorientation of the para-commando troops themselves, the Belgian Special Forces will be reinforced both quantitatively and qualitatively, and in the future will have specialised air assets at their disposal (Special Forces Aviation

[542] The entire Special Operations Forces capability will be able to contribute to all core tasks, but it is especially a clear reinforcement of the Belgian possibilities to contribute to NATO’s new deployment concepts within the framework of collective defence

[543] This is further emphasised by the participation of Belgian Defence in the NATO project for a Composite Special Operations Component Command (C-SOCC), which will be created together with the Netherlands and Denmark

[544] For Belgian Defence this contribution will be based on a national Special Operations Command (SOCOM

[545] An important basic mission of the Special Forces themselves as well as of their Support Group (the Rangers) is providing local ground troops in an armed conflict with military assistance

[546] This military deployment form is increasingly preferred to an active armed external intervention and plays an ever more important role in crisis management operations within the framework of collective security

[547] The particularity of airborne units is that they are deployed from the air by parachute, as well as from an aircraft or helicopter

[548] In the case of Belgium especially by parachute and from an aircraft (assault landing

[549] The tactical advantage of this deployment method is the surprise effect

[550] It involves light troops with limited deployment period, limited operational mobility, and little protection and firepower

[551] Ever more effective air defence systems and well-equipped opponents have made this method of deployment very risky

[552] As a result, the deployment of airborne troops in armed conflicts is therefore currently very limited

[553] The feasibility to deploy these units has already been improved by reinforcing their motorised character through the recent decision to equip the troops with Rapid Reaction Vehicles

[554] Operators of our Special Forces are training together with a German helicopter and a Norwegian submarine during a NATO exercise in Norway

[555] The Special Operations Forces capability consists of the Special Forces (Special Forces Group), the Special Forces Support Group (2 Ranger battalions) and the Special Forces Aviation

[556] The deployment of combined arms motorised land forces (including their own combat support and combat service support elements) requires additional essential support capabilities

[557] Our important strategic air transport capability is an element in ensuring the expeditionary deployment of these troops

[558] The future A400M transport aircraft and a possible participation in the Joint Logistic Support Ship will reinforce the expeditionary deployment capability and their logistic and medical support

[559] Other air support assets, such as combat aircraft and drones, will allow for additional precision firepower as well as the necessary intelligence gathering to obtain a good situational awareness

[560] Teams for chemical, biological, radiological and nuclear warfare, explosive ordnance disposal teams and military construction teams contribute to a safe deployment of the motorised land forces, as well as to other forms of expeditionary deployment

[561] Helicopters can supply additional mobility and can be deployed for medical support of ground troops

[562] A nonkinetic capability such as influence support teams contributes to the engagement of the combined arms motorised capability (as well as of other capabilities), allowing the deployed troops to better understand and work with the local population and execute influence operations

[563] In combination with military construction, these teams can provide the population with humanitarian assistance in support of the military deployment and revive social aid

[564] This non-kinetic support capability is also essential to the development of an effective comprehensive approach in an engagement zone

[565] The Strategic Vision for Defence – 29 June 2016 See chapter 5, section a, paragraph vi giving examples for the Influence capability

[566] The cyber environment is an essential environment for the success of the military operational deployment

[567] Strengthening our own military cyber capability is a necessity

[568] Military Cyber Capability: Essential for Deployment Within the Framework of Collective Security and Collective Defence A military cyber capability contributes to the defence of communication and weapon systems that are crucial to armed forces, but it also has to be able to participate in offensive actions within the expeditionary deployment

[569] In addition to the contribution of cyber capabilities to the deployment within the framework of collective security, it should also be pointed out that since 2014 these capabilities have been recognised as essential capabilities for NATO’s collective defence

[570] The NATO summit in Wales added the cyber dimension to the dimensions in which NATO exercises collective defence, as it has been noticed that cyber attacks can have the same effect on the territorial integrity of NATO countries as conventional attacks

[571] Therefore, our military cyber capability also needs to be reinforced to meet the needs of collective defence

[572] NATO considers this a national responsibility, but expects the countries that have important NATO facilities on their territory to take the lead in the matter of cyber protection

[573] A Modern Approach to the Core Task ‘Protection of Belgian Citizens Worldwide’ Our citizens live all over the world

[574] The safety of these Belgians is ensured in the first place by the contribution of the Belgian authorities to security in the world, notably through diplomatic action, development cooperation and the deployment of Defence, mainly within the context of the core task of ‘collective security’

[575] However, situations can occur in which there is a direct threat against Belgian citizens or groups of Belgians in a particular country, region or city, or against individual Belgian citizens who perform specific duties

[576] When local authorities are no longer capable of ensuring the safety of our fellow citizens, Defence must be able to intervene in order to ensure their physical safety worldwide

[577] When Belgian citizens abroad are faced with a direct threat, this is identified in first instance by our military intelligence service, which is in close contact with the Belgian diplomacy and consular services, the Belgian intelligence and security services, and the intelligence services of partner nations

[578] This service provides the Belgian authorities with intelligence and makes it possible for the government to take appropriate measures

[579] The cyber dimension has been added to the dimensions in which NATO exercises collective defence, as it has been noticed that cyber attacks can have the same effect on the territorial integrity of NATO countries as conventional attacks

[580] T h e S t r a t e

[581] i s i o n f o r D e f e n c e – 2 9 J u n e

[582] In most cases the threat against Belgian citizens abroad builds gradually, thus allowing the Belgian authorities to react in stages, if possible in consultation with other countries and at the European level

[583] The Consular services are responsible for informing Belgian citizens under threat, encouraging them to take appropriate measures, such as leaving the country by civilian transport before the security situation deteriorates further

[584] However, at a certain moment the authorities can decide that an effective and immediate evacuation of national citizens is necessary because their safety can no longer be guaranteed

[585] Defence can provide transport with its strategic means of transport when the civilian transport no longer works

[586] At the moment, our strategic means of transport consist of air transport only

[587] In the future, this could possibly be expanded with strategic maritime transport

[588] If there are indications that the security situation in a specific region can change dramatically and that the safety of our citizens is in danger, the authorities can decide to preposition soldiers and equipment for a fast intervention

[589] Prepositioning can be organised on land or, possibly in the future, at sea through the JSS and an amphibious company

[590] Prepositioning takes into account the fact that because of the current proliferation of war weapons, potential opponents can have relatively heavy weaponry at their disposal, which would entail that evacuation operations require armoured motorised assets, if necessary even supported by air assets

[591] In case of imminent or direct threats against our citizens, without the possibility to preposition, Defence will continue to be able to execute a military evacuation of citizens (Non-Combatant Evacuation Operation - NEO

[592] A flexible detachment adapted to the operational assessment, ranging from a reinforcement of the security teams on the spot to a SOF Task Force, must be able to deploy within that context by means of air transport assets

[593] In the future, the actual organisation of evacuation operations will be increasingly international and multilateral, for which France is and remains the most evident strategic partner

[594] This partnership is reflected, among other things, in the Belgian participation in the command structure of the Rapid Reaction Corps in Lille

[595] Belgium provides an evacuation contribution within an international framework with a focus on Sub-Saharan Africa and the Great Lakes region for historical reasons, whereas other countries take the lead in other regions

[596] Our military capabilities should also be able to contribute to evacuation operations around the world, possibly under the leadership of other countries

[597] Besides evacuation operations aimed at repatriating large groups of people, Belgian Defence also has to be able to execute direct actions across the world when Belgian citizens are in danger, if necessary, to free Belgian hostages

[598] The maximum configuration of a SOF Task Force consists of one SOF Task Group and one Ranger battalion

[599] In the future, the actual organisation of evacuation operations will be increasingly international and multilateral

[600] T h e S t r a t e

[601] Special Forces operators can also ensure the protection of Belgian citizens whose job requires them to stay in unsafe areas, as is mainly the case for embassy staff

[602] In addition, they also carry out from time to time Close Protection missions to protect delegations

[603] c. Optimisation of Support Provided by Defence to Internal Security Internal security is not one of Defence’s core tasks

[604] However, Defence plans to support internal security, for there is an obvious interconnection between internal and external security, as has become very clear with the increased terrorist threat on our territory

[605] Ideally, our security is ensured as far away from our borders as possible through expeditionary deployment within the framework of collective security

[606] Today however, it is a fact that terrorism linked to other security issues constitutes a threat to our security, including that on our own territory

[607] In addition, the direct consequences of global warming will cause more extreme weather, and thus potentially lead to an increased deployment of Defence for consequence management of disasters

[608] In view of the permanent reality that Defence is the last bulwark for the security of our society, it will optimise the use of its capabilities – set up around the three core tasks – within the framework of internal security

[609] That being said, Defence already carries out internal security missions in a structural way

[610] On the one hand, it makes the specialist assets available that are covering the territorial aspect of collective defence for a role in internal security

[611] On the other hand, Defence takes up some territorial tasks because of its (historical) expertise (Explosive Ordnance Disposal Service (EODS), Search and Rescue helicopters, national missions of the military intelligence service, some capabilities of the military medical support

[612] Two combat aircraft are permanently available to NATO on the national territory for Quick Reaction Alert missions (air defence) and can also be deployed, for reasons of interdepartmental efficiency, for missions within the framework of internal security (air policing), such as the counter-terrorism Renegade mission

[613] The same goes for the Navy’s coastal patrol vessels that are always on standby to follow up any military threats in our maritime area of responsibility

[614] These vessels can also execute tasks within the framework of internal security, such as fisheries control, the fight against trafficking (drugs, human Renegade: the deployment of combat aircraft for the interception, identification, diversion and – as a last resort – neutralisation of civil aircraft that can be used as terrorist weapons, as during the attacks of 11 September

[615] In view of the permanent reality that Defence is the last bulwark for the security of our society, it will optimise the use of its capabilities – set up around the three core tasks – within the framework of internal security
